fincen-priorities-statement-nonbank

Statement on the Issuance of AML/CFT Priorities for non-bank financial institutions · https://www.fincen.gov/system/files/shared/Statement%20for%20Non-Bank%20Financial%20Institutions%20(June%2030%2C%202021).pdf
Actor / modalityObligation
mustthe Secretary of the Treasury (in consultation with the Attorney General, Federal functional regulators, relevant State financial regulators, and relevant national security agencies)establish and make public priorities for anti-money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism policy (AML/CFT Priorities)
✓ “The Anti-Money Laundering Act of 2020 (the “ AML Act”)1 requires the Secretary of the Treasury, in consultation with the Attorney General, Federal functional regulators, 2 relevant State financial regulators, and relevant national security agencies, to establish and make public priorities for anti-money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism policy (AML/CFT Priorities).”
mustFinCEN (in consultation with the Federal functional regulators and relevant State financial regulators)as appropriate, promulgate regulations regarding the AML/CFT Priorities
trigger: within 180 days of the establishment of the AML/CFT Priorities
threshold: within 180 days
exceptions: as appropriate
✓ “The AML Act requires that, within 180 days of the establishment of the AML/CFT Priorities, FinCEN (in consultation with the Federal functional regulators and relevant State financial regulators) shall, as appropriate, promulgate regulations regarding the AML/CFT Priorities.”
conditionalcovered NBFIsincorporate the AML/CFT Priorities into their risk-based AML programs
trigger: the effective date of the final regulations
exceptions: not required until the effective date of the final regulations
✓ “Covered NBFIs are not required to incorporate the AML/CFT Priorities into their risk-based AML programs until the effective date of the final regulations.”
maycovered NBFIsstart considering how they will incorporate the AML/CFT Priorities into their risk-based AML programs, such as by assessing the potential risks associated with the products and services they offer, the customers they serve, and the geographic areas in which they operate
trigger: in preparation for any new requirements when those final rules are published
✓ “covered NBFIs may wish to start considering how they will incorporate the AML/CFT Priorities into their risk-based AML programs, such as by assessing the potential risks associated with the products and services they offer, the customers they serve, and the geographic areas in which they operate.”
mustcovered NBFI (AML Act requirement)have its review of the AML/CFT Priorities and the incorporation of those Priorities, as appropriate, into its risk-based AML program included as a measure on which it is supervised and examined
exceptions: as appropriate
✓ “the AML Act requires that the review by a covered NBFI of the AML/CFT Priorities and the incorporation of those Priorities, as appropriate, into its risk-based AML program, be included as a measure on which it is supervised and examined.”
must_notFinCENexamine covered NBFIs for the incorporation of the AML/CFT Priorities into their risk-based AML programs
trigger: until the effective date of final regulations
exceptions: applies until the effective date of final regulations
✓ “This statement confirms that FinCEN will not examine covered NBFIs for the incorporation of the AML/CFT Priorities into their risk-based AML programs until the effective date of final regulations noted above.”
must_notFinCENrequest that the staff of any of the Agencies, or a self-regulatory organization (SRO) authorized to examine a covered NBFI, examine any covered NBFIs for this requirement (or any related state requirement)
trigger: until the effective date of final regulations
exceptions: applies until the effective date of final regulations
✓ “FinCEN will not request that the staff of any of the Agencies, or a self-regulatory organization (SRO) authorized to examine a covered NBFI, examine any covered NBFIs for this requirement (or any related state requirement) until the effective date of final regulations.”
TermDefinition (as given)
AML ActThe Anti-Money Laundering Act of 2020, enacted as Division F, §§ 6001-6511, of the William M. (Mac) Thornberry National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2021, H.R. 6395, 116th Cong. (2021).
✓ “The AML Act was enacted as Division F, §§ 6001-6511, of the William M. (Mac) Thornberry National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2021, H.R. 6395, 116th Cong. (2021) (enacted)”
Federal functional regulatorAs defined in section 509 of the Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act (15 U.S.C. 6809), per 31 U.S.C. § 5318(h)(4)(A) (as amended by AML Act § 6101(b)(2)(C)).
✓ “31 U.S.C. § 5318(h)(4)(A) (as amended by AML Act § 6101(b)(2)(C)) uses the term Federal functional regulator “as defined in section 509 of the Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act (15 U.S.C. 6809)).””
the AgenciesFinCEN in consultation with the staff of the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, Commodity Futures Trading Commission, Internal Revenue Service, and State financial regulators (collectively).
✓ “FinCEN, in consultation with the staff of the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission, Commodity Futures Trading Commission, Internal Revenue Service, and State financial regulators (collectively, “the Agencies”)”
covered NBFIsAll non-bank financial institutions (NBFIs) and other entities with regulatory AML program requirements (collectively), cross-referenced to 31 CFR §§ 1020.210(b), 1021.210, 1022.210, 1023.210, 1024.210, 1025.210, 1026.210, 1027.210, 1028.210, 1029.210, and 1030.210.
✓ “all non-bank financial institutions (NBFIs) and other entities with regulatory AML program requirements (collectively “covered NBFIs”)4”
federal banking agencies (FBAs)The Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (collectively).
✓ “the Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System, the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency (collectively, the “federal banking agencies” or “FBAs”)”

Rules for Banks · 31 CFR Part 1020
Actor / modalityObligation
conditionalA bank regulated by a Federal functional regulatorImplement and maintain an anti-money laundering program that complies with §§ 1010.610 and 1010.620, includes a system of internal controls, independent testing, a designated compliance coordinator, training, and appropriate risk-based CDD procedures, and complies with its Federal functional regulator's program regulation, in order to be deemed to satisfy 31 U.S.C. 5318(h)(1).
trigger: Bank is regulated by a Federal functional regulator and seeks to satisfy 31 U.S.C. 5318(h)(1)
✓ “A bank regulated by a Federal functional regulator shall be deemed to satisfy the requirements of 31 U.S.C. 5318(h)(1) if it implements and maintains an anti-money laundering program that:”
✓ “A system of internal controls to assure ongoing compliance;”
✓ “Independent testing for compliance to be conducted by bank personnel or by an outside party;”
✓ “Designation of an individual or individuals responsible for coordinating and monitoring day-to-day compliance;”
mustA bank regulated by a Federal functional regulatorMaintain appropriate risk-based procedures for conducting ongoing customer due diligence, including understanding the nature and purpose of customer relationships to develop a customer risk profile and conducting ongoing monitoring to identify and report suspicious transactions and, on a risk basis, maintain and update customer information including beneficial owners of legal entity customers.
trigger: Bank maintains an AML program under § 1020.210(a)
✓ “Understanding the nature and purpose of customer relationships for the purpose of developing a customer risk profile; and”
✓ “Conducting ongoing monitoring to identify and report suspicious transactions and, on a risk basis, to maintain and update customer information.”
conditionalA bank lacking a Federal functional regulator (including private banks, non-federally insured credit unions, certain trust companies)Establish and maintain a written anti-money laundering program that complies with §§ 1010.610 and 1010.620 and includes internal controls, independent testing, a designated compliance coordinator, training, and risk-based CDD procedures, in order to be deemed to satisfy 31 U.S.C. 5318(h)(1).
trigger: Bank lacks a Federal functional regulator and seeks to satisfy 31 U.S.C. 5318(h)(1)

mustA bank lacking a Federal functional regulatorHave its written anti-money laundering program approved by the board of directors or, if none, an equivalent governing body.

mustA bank required to have an anti-money laundering compliance program under regulations implementing 31 U.S.C. 5318(h), 12 U.S.C. 1818(s), or 12 U.S.C. 1786(q)(1)Implement a written Customer Identification Program (CIP) appropriate for the bank's size and type of business, meeting the minimum requirements of paragraphs (a)(1) through (5), and the CIP must be a part of the anti-money laundering compliance program.
trigger: Bank is required to have an AML compliance program
✓ “must implement a written Customer Identification Program (CIP) appropriate for the bank's size and type of business that, at a minimum, includes each of the requirements of paragraphs (a)(1) through (5) of this section.”
✓ “The CIP must be a part of the anti-money laundering compliance program.”
mustBank (CIP)Include risk-based procedures for verifying the identity of each customer to the extent reasonable and practicable, enabling the bank to form a reasonable belief that it knows the true identity of each customer, based on the bank's risk assessment.
✓ “The CIP must include risk-based procedures for verifying the identity of each customer to the extent reasonable and practicable.”
✓ “The procedures must enable the bank to form a reasonable belief that it knows the true identity of each customer.”
mustBank (CIP)Obtain, prior to opening an account, at minimum the customer's name, date of birth (for an individual), address, and identification number (TIN for U.S. persons; TIN, passport, alien ID, or other government-issued document for non-U.S. persons).
trigger: Prior to opening an account
exceptions: Exception for persons who have applied for but not received a taxpayer identification number (§1020.220(a)(2)(i)(B)); Credit card accounts may obtain identifying information from a third-party source prior to extending credit (§1020.220(a)(2)(i)(C))

mustBank (CIP)Contain procedures for verifying the identity of the customer within a reasonable time after the account is opened, using documents, non-documentary methods, or a combination.
trigger: Within a reasonable time after account opened
✓ “The CIP must contain procedures for verifying the identity of the customer, using information obtained in accordance with paragraph (a)(2)(i) of this section, within a reasonable time after the account is opened.”
mustBank (CIP)Include procedures for responding to circumstances in which the bank cannot form a reasonable belief that it knows the true identity of a customer, describing when not to open, restrict use of, or close an account, and when to file a SAR.
trigger: Bank cannot form reasonable belief it knows customer's true identity
✓ “The CIP must include procedures for responding to circumstances in which the bank cannot form a reasonable belief that it knows the true identity of a customer.”
mustBank (CIP)Include procedures for making and maintaining a record of all information obtained under CIP identification and verification procedures.
✓ “The CIP must include procedures for making and maintaining a record of all information obtained under the procedures implementing paragraph (a) of this section.”
mustBank (CIP)Retain identifying information for five years after the account is closed (or, for credit card accounts, five years after closure or dormancy); retain document description/verification/discrepancy-resolution records for five years after the record is made.
trigger: Account closed / record made
threshold: five years
✓ “The bank must retain the information in paragraph (a)(3)(i)(A) of this section for five years after the date the account is closed or, in the case of credit card accounts, five years after the account is closed or becomes dormant.”
mustBank (CIP)Include procedures for determining whether the customer appears on any Treasury-designated list of known or suspected terrorists or terrorist organizations, within a reasonable period after the account is opened (or earlier if required), and follow all related Federal directives.
trigger: Account opened

mustBank (CIP)Include procedures for providing customers with adequate notice that the bank is requesting information to verify their identities.
✓ “The CIP must include procedures for providing bank customers with adequate notice that the bank is requesting information to verify their identities.”
mayBank (CIP)Include procedures specifying reliance on another financial institution's performance of CIP procedures for a shared customer, subject to reasonableness, the other institution being subject to a rule implementing 5318(h) and regulated by a Federal functional regulator, and an annual certification contract.
✓ “The CIP may include procedures specifying when a bank will rely on the performance by another financial institution (including an affiliate) of any procedures of the bank's CIP”

✓ “The appropriate Federal functional regulator, with the concurrence of the Secretary, may, by order or regulation, exempt any bank or type of account from the requirements of this section.”
TermDefinition (as given)
Account (for purposes of § 1020.220)A formal banking relationship established to provide or engage in services, dealings, or other financial transactions including a deposit account, a transaction or asset account, a credit account, or other extension of credit; also includes safety deposit box, safekeeping, cash management, custodian, and trust service relationships. Excludes relationships without a formal banking relationship (e.g., check-cashing, wire transfer, sale of a check or money order), accounts acquired via acquisition/merger/purchase of assets/assumption of liabilities, and accounts opened for participation in an ERISA employee benefit plan.
✓ “Account means a formal banking relationship established to provide or engage in services, dealings, or other financial transactions including a deposit account, a transaction or asset account, a credit account, or other extension of credit.”
✓ “Account also includes a relationship established to provide a safety deposit box or other safekeeping services, or cash management, custodian, and trust services.”
Customer (for purposes of § 1020.220)A person that opens a new account, and an individual who opens a new account for an individual lacking legal capacity or for an entity that is not a legal person. Excludes a financial institution regulated by a Federal functional regulator or a bank regulated by a State bank regulator, persons described in § 1020.315(b)(2)-(4), and a person with an existing account where the bank reasonably believes it knows the person's true identity.

✓ “A financial institution regulated by a Federal functional regulator or a bank regulated by a State bank regulator;”

✗ “customer information shall include information regarding the beneficial owners of legal entity customers (as defined in § 1010.230 of this chapter)”

maybroker-dealerinclude procedures specifying reliance on another financial institution's performance of CIP procedures for a shared customer
exceptions: Such reliance is reasonable under the circumstances; The other financial institution is subject to a rule implementing 31 U.S.C. 5318(h), and regulated by a Federal functional regulator; The other financial institution enters into a contract requiring it to certify annually to the broker-dealer that it has implemented its anti-money laundering program, and that it will perform specified requirements of the broker-dealer's CIP
✓ “The CIP may include procedures specifying when the broker-dealer will rely on the performance by another financial institution (including an affiliate) of any procedures of the broker-dealer's CIP”
mustbroker-dealerfile with FinCEN a report of any suspicious transaction relevant to a possible violation of law or regulation
trigger: conducted or attempted by, at, or through a broker-dealer, involving or aggregating funds or other assets of at least $5,000, and the broker-dealer knows, suspects, or has reason to suspect specified categories of suspicion
threshold: $5,000
exceptions: robbery or burglary reported to law enforcement or 17 CFR 240.17f-1 filings; certain federal securities law/SRO-rule violations appropriately reported to SEC or SRO (other than 17 CFR 240.17a-8 or 405.4)
✗ “Every broker or dealer in securities within the United States (for purposes of this section, a “broker-dealer”) shall file with FinCEN, to the extent and in the manner required by this section, a report of any suspicious transaction relevant to a possible violation of law or regulation.”
✓ “A transaction requires reporting under the terms of this section if it is conducted or attempted by, at, or through a broker-dealer, it involves or aggregates funds or other assets of at least $5,000, and the broker-dealer knows, suspects, or has reason to suspect that the transaction”
mustbroker-dealerfile a SAR no later than 30 calendar days after initial detection of facts that may constitute a basis for filing, with a possible additional 30-day delay to identify a suspect, but no more than 60 calendar days total
trigger: initial detection by the reporting broker-dealer of facts that may constitute a basis for filing a SAR
threshold: 30 calendar days (up to 60 calendar days if no suspect identified)
✓ “A SAR shall be filed no later than 30 calendar days after the date of the initial detection by the reporting broker-dealer of facts that may constitute a basis for filing a SAR under this section.”
✓ “but in no case shall reporting be delayed more than 60 calendar days after the date of such initial detection.”
mustbroker-dealerimmediately notify by telephone an appropriate law enforcement authority in addition to filing a timely SAR
trigger: situations involving violations that require immediate attention, such as terrorist financing or ongoing money laundering schemes
✓ “In situations involving violations that require immediate attention, such as terrorist financing or ongoing money laundering schemes, the broker-dealer shall immediately notify by telephone an appropriate law enforcement authority in addition to filing timely a SAR.”
mustbroker-dealermaintain a copy of any SAR filed and supporting documentation for five years from the date of filing, and make supporting documentation available upon request to FinCEN, law enforcement, Federal regulatory authorities, or (via SEC request) an SRO
threshold: five years from the date of filing
✓ “A broker-dealer shall maintain a copy of any SAR filed and the original or business record equivalent of any supporting documentation for a period of five years from the date of filing the SAR.”
must_notbroker-dealer; director, officer, employee, or agent of any broker-dealerdisclose a SAR or any information that would reveal the existence of a SAR, except as authorized
exceptions: disclosure to FinCEN or Federal/State/local law enforcement or Federal regulatory authority examining for BSA compliance, or to an SRO examining for compliance upon SEC request; disclosure of underlying facts/transactions/documents for a joint SAR or per 31 U.S.C. 5318(g)(2)(B); sharing within corporate organizational structure consistent with Title II of the BSA
✓ “No broker-dealer, and no director, officer, employee, or agent of any broker-dealer, shall disclose a SAR or any information that would reveal the existence of a SAR.”
mustbroker-dealer; director, officer, employee, or agentdecline to produce a subpoenaed or requested SAR or SAR-revealing information, citing this section and 31 U.S.C. 5318(g)(2)(A)(i), and notify FinCEN of the request and response
trigger: subpoenaed or otherwise requested to disclose a SAR or information revealing its existence
✓ “Any broker-dealer, and any director, officer, employee, or agent of any broker-dealer that is subpoenaed or otherwise requested to disclose a SAR or any information that would reveal the existence of a SAR, shall decline to produce the SAR or such information, citing this section and 31 U.S.C. 5318(g)(2)(A)(i), and shall notify FinCEN of any such request and the response thereto.”
must_notFederal, State, local, territorial, or Tribal government authority, or director/officer/employee/agent thereofdisclose a SAR or SAR-revealing information except as necessary to fulfill official duties consistent with Title II of the BSA
exceptions: necessary to fulfill official duties consistent with Title II of the Bank Secrecy Act
✓ “A Federal, State, local, territorial, or Tribal government authority, or any director, officer, employee, or agent of any of the foregoing, shall not disclose a SAR, or any information that would reveal the existence of a SAR, except as necessary to fulfill official duties consistent with Title II of the Bank Secrecy Act.”
must_notself-regulatory organization registered with the SEC, or director/officer/employee/agent thereofdisclose a SAR or SAR-revealing information except as necessary to fulfill self-regulatory duties with SEC consent, consistent with Title II of the BSA
exceptions: necessary to fulfill self-regulatory duties with the consent of the SEC, consistent with Title II of the BSA
✓ “Any self-regulatory organization registered with the Securities and Exchange Commission, or any director, officer, employee, or agent of any of the foregoing, shall not disclose a SAR, or any information that would reveal the existence of a SAR except as necessary to fulfill self-regulatory duties with the consent of the Securities Exchange Commission, in a manner consistent with Title II of the Bank Secrecy Act.”
mustbroker-dealersecure and maintain a record of taxpayer identification number (or social security number for individual account holders) within 30 days of opening a brokerage account
trigger: each brokerage account opened after June 30, 1972 and before October 1, 2003 by a U.S. resident, business, or citizen
threshold: within 30 days from the date such account is opened
exceptions: reasonable-effort/list exception where identification could not be secured; enumerated categories in (a)(3)(i)-(vi) where a TIN need not be secured; extension where person has applied via Form SS-4 or SS-5
✓ “such broker or dealer shall within 30 days from the date such account is opened, secure and maintain a record of the taxpayer identification number of the person maintaining the account”
mustbroker or dealer in securitiesretain the original or a copy/reproduction of specified documents: signature/trading authority documents, records described in 17 CFR 240.17a-3(a), and records of remittances/transfers and receipts exceeding $10,000 to/from persons, accounts, or places outside the United States
threshold: more than $10,000
✓ “Every broker or dealer in securities shall, in addition, retain either the original or a microfilm or other copy or reproduction of each of the following:”
✓ “A record of each remittance or transfer of funds, or of currency, checks, other monetary instruments, investment securities, or credit, of more than $10,000 to a person, account, or place, outside the United States;”

✓ “Provide for annual (on a calendar-year basis) independent testing for compliance to be conducted by member personnel or by a qualified outside party, unless the member does not execute transactions for customers or otherwise hold customer accounts or act as an introducing broker with respect to customer accounts (e.g., engages solely in proprietary trading or conducts business only with other broker-dealers), in which case such "independent testing" is required every two years (on a calendar-year basis);”

FINRA-RN-17-40

Guidance Regarding AML Program Requirements Under FINRA Rule 3310 Following Adoption of FinCEN's CDD Rule · RN 17-40
Actor / modalityObligation
mustFINRA member firmdevelop and implement a written AML program reasonably designed to achieve and monitor the member firm's compliance with the BSA and implementing regulations
✓ “FINRA Rule 3310 requires each member firm to develop and implement a written AML program reasonably designed to achieve and monitor the member firm's compliance with the BSA and implementing regulations.”
mustFINRA member firmestablish and implement policies and procedures that can be reasonably expected to detect and cause the reporting of suspicious transactions
✓ “(1) establish and implement policies and procedures that can be reasonably expected to detect and cause the reporting of suspicious transactions;”
mustFINRA member firmestablish and implement policies, procedures, and internal controls reasonably designed to achieve compliance with the BSA and implementing regulations
✓ “(2) establish and implement policies, procedures, and internal controls reasonably designed to achieve compliance with the BSA and implementing regulations;”
mustFINRA member firmprovide for annual (calendar-year basis) independent testing for compliance conducted by member firm personnel or a qualified outside party
threshold: annual, calendar-year basis
exceptions: If a member firm does not execute transactions for customers or otherwise hold customer accounts or act as an introducing broker with respect to customer accounts (e.g., engages solely in proprietary trading or conducts business only with other broker-dealers), then "independent testing" is required every two years.
✓ “(3) provide for annual (on a calendar-year basis) independent testing for compliance to be conducted by member firm personnel or a qualified outside party;10”
✓ “If a member firm does not execute transactions for customers or otherwise hold customer accounts or act as an introducing broker with respect to customer accounts (e.g., engages solely in proprietary trading or conducts business only with other broker-dealers), then "independent testing" is required every two years.”
mustFINRA member firmdesignate and identify to FINRA an individual or individuals (AML compliance person(s)) responsible for implementing and monitoring the day-to-day operations and internal controls of the AML program, and provide prompt notification to FINRA of any changes to the designation
trigger: any change to the designation
✓ “(4) designate and identify to FINRA an individual or individuals (i.e., AML compliance person(s)) who will be responsible for implementing and monitoring the day-to-day operations and internal controls of the AML program and provide prompt notification to FINRA of any changes to the designation;”
mustFINRA member firmprovide ongoing training for appropriate persons
✓ “(5) provide ongoing training for appropriate persons.”
mustmember firm (covered financial institution broker-dealer)continue to comply with FINRA Rule 3310's requirements notwithstanding adoption of the CDD Rule, because the CDD Rule does not change FINRA Rule 3310's requirements
✓ “FinCEN's CDD Rule does not change the requirements of FINRA Rule 3310, and member firms must continue to comply with its requirements.11”
mustmember firm (covered financial institution broker-dealer)be in compliance with the CDD Rule's provisions
threshold: by May 11, 2018
✓ “Member firms must be in compliance with its provisions by May 11, 2018.”
shouldmember firmensure that their AML programs are updated, as necessary, to comply with the CDD Rule
threshold: by May 11, 2018
✓ “member firms should ensure that their AML programs are updated, as necessary, to comply with the CDD Rule by May 11, 2018.”
mustmember firm (covered financial institution broker-dealer)include risk-based procedures for conducting ongoing customer due diligence in AML programs, comprising (1) understanding the nature and purpose of customer relationships for developing a customer risk profile and (2) conducting ongoing monitoring to identify and report suspicious transactions and, on a risk basis, to maintain and update customer information
✓ “FinCEN's CDD Rule amends the minimum statutory requirements for member firms' AML programs by requiring such programs to include risk-based procedures for conducting ongoing customer due diligence.12”
✓ “This ongoing customer due diligence element, or "fifth pillar" required for AML programs, includes: (1) understanding the nature and purpose of customer relationships for the purpose of developing a customer risk profile; and (2) conducting ongoing monitoring to identify and report suspicious transactions and, on a risk basis, to maintain and update customer information.”
conditionalmember firmupdate the customer information, including information regarding the beneficial owners of legal entity customers
trigger: if, in the course of its normal monitoring for suspicious activity, the member firm detects information that is relevant to assessing the customer's risk profile
exceptions: there is no expectation that the member firm update customer information, including beneficial ownership information, on an ongoing or continuous basis
✓ “If, in the course of its normal monitoring for suspicious activity, the member firm detects information that is relevant to assessing the customer's risk profile, the member firm must update the customer information, including the information regarding the beneficial owners of legal entity customers, as discussed below.23”
✓ “However, there is no expectation that the member firm update customer information, including beneficial ownership information, on an ongoing or continuous basis.24”
mustmember firm (covered financial institution broker-dealer)establish and maintain written procedures as part of AML programs that are reasonably designed to identify and verify the identities of beneficial owners of legal entity customers
✓ “the CDD Rule also requires member firms to establish and maintain written procedures as part of their AML programs that are reasonably designed to identify and verify the identities of beneficial owners25 of legal entity customers.26”
mustmember firmobtain from the natural person opening the account on behalf of the legal entity customer the identity of the beneficial owners of the entity, and that individual must certify, to the best of his or her knowledge, as to the accuracy of the information
trigger: opening a new account on behalf of a legal entity customer
✓ “Under the CDD Rule, member firms must obtain from the natural person opening the account29 on behalf of the legal entity customer, the identity of the beneficial owners of the entity.30”
✓ “In addition, that individual must certify, to the best of his or her knowledge, as to the accuracy of the information.”
mustmember firmmaintain records of the beneficial ownership information they obtain
✓ “In any case, the CDD Rule requires that member firms maintain records of the beneficial ownership information they obtain.35”
mustmember firmverify the identity of the beneficial owner(s) through risk-based procedures that include, at a minimum, the elements required for member firms' CIP procedures for verifying the identity of individual customers, completed within a reasonable time after account opening
trigger: after obtaining required beneficial ownership information
threshold: within a reasonable time after account opening
✓ “Once member firms obtain the required beneficial ownership information, the CDD Rule requires that member firms verify the identity of the beneficial owner(s)—in other words, that they are who they say they are and not their status as beneficial owners—through riskbased procedures that include, at a minimum, the elements required for member firms' CIP procedures for verifying the identity of individual customers.36”
✓ “Such verification must be completed within a reasonable time after account opening.37”
maymember firmrely on the beneficial ownership information supplied by the individual opening the account
exceptions: provided that they have no knowledge of facts that would reasonably call into question the reliability of that information
✓ “Member firms may rely on the beneficial ownership information supplied by the individual opening the account, provided that they have no knowledge of facts that would reasonably call into question the reliability of that information.38”
maymember firmrely on another financial institution for the performance of the CDD Rule's requirements, to the same extent as permitted under the CIP rules
✓ “To the same extent as permitted under the CIP rules, the CDD Rule permits member firms to rely on another financial institution for the performance of the CDD Rule's requirements.39”
conditionalmember firmobtain beneficial ownership information for an existing legal entity customer
trigger: if, during the course of normal monitoring, the firm receives information that is needed to assess or reevaluate the risk of the customer
✓ “However, a member firm should obtain beneficial ownership information for an existing legal entity customer if, during the course of normal monitoring, it receives information that is needed to assess or reevaluate the risk of the customer.40”
TermDefinition (as given)
four pillarsThe statutorily enumerated minimum requirements for AML programs: (1) establishment and implementation of policies, procedures and internal controls reasonably designed to achieve compliance with the applicable provisions of the BSA and implementing regulations; (2) independent testing for compliance by broker-dealer personnel or a qualified outside party; (3) designation of an individual or individuals responsible for implementing and monitoring the operations and internal controls of the AML program; and (4) ongoing training for appropriate persons.
✓ “These four pillars require brokerdealers to have written AML programs that include, at a minimum:”
fifth pillarA new requirement, added by the CDD Rule, explicitly requiring AML programs to include risk-based procedures for conducting ongoing customer due diligence, including: (1) understanding the nature and purpose of customer relationships for the purpose of developing a customer risk profile; and (2) conducting ongoing monitoring to identify and report suspicious transactions and, on a risk basis, to maintain and update customer information.
✓ “by amending the existing AML program rules for covered financial institutions to explicitly require these components to be included in AML programs as a new "fifth pillar."”
✓ “This ongoing customer due diligence element, or "fifth pillar" required for AML programs, includes: (1) understanding the nature and purpose of customer relationships for the purpose of developing a customer risk profile; and (2) conducting ongoing monitoring to identify and report suspicious transactions and, on a risk basis, to maintain and update customer information.”
customer risk profileInformation gathered about a customer to form the baseline against which customer activity is assessed for suspicious transaction reporting.
✓ “The customer risk profile refers to information gathered about a customer to form the baseline against which customer activity is assessed for suspicious transaction reporting.16”
beneficial ownerThere are both ownership and control prongs of the definition of beneficial owner for purposes of the CDD Rule. A beneficial owner is: (1) each individual (if any) who directly or indirectly owns 25 percent of the equity interests of a legal entity customer; and (2) a single individual with significant responsibility to control, manage, or direct a legal entity customer, including an executive officer or senior manager.
✓ “A beneficial owner is: (1) each individual (if any) who directly or indirectly owns 25 percent of the equity interests of a legal entity customer; and (2) a single individual with significant responsibility to control, manage, or direct a legal entity customer, including an executive officer or senior manager.”
legal entity customerA "corporation, limited liability company, or other entity that is created by the filing of a public document with a Secretary of State or similar office, a general partnership, and any similar entity formed under the laws of a foreign jurisdiction, that opens an account."
✓ “A legal entity customer is a "corporation, limited liability company, or other entity that is created by the filing of a public document with a Secretary of State or similar office, a general partnership, and any similar entity formed under the laws of a foreign jurisdiction, that opens an account." 31 CFR 1010.230(e)(1).”
covered financial institution(undefined here; term used with cross-reference to 31 CFR 1010.230(f))
✓ “See 31 CFR. 1010.230(f) (defining "covered financial institution").”
financial institution(undefined here; term used with cross-reference to 31 U.S.C. 5312(a)(2))
✓ “See 31 U.S.C. 5312(a)(2) (defining "financial institution").”
account(undefined here; the CDD Rule incorporates the definition of "account" used in the CIP rules)
✓ “The CDD Rule incorporates the definition of "account" that is used in the CIP rules. See 31 CFR 1010.230(c).”
AML compliance person(s)(undefined here) individual(s) designated and identified to FINRA who will be responsible for implementing and monitoring the day-to-day operations and internal controls of the AML program.
✓ “(4) designate and identify to FINRA an individual or individuals (i.e., AML compliance person(s)) who will be responsible for implementing and monitoring the day-to-day operations and internal controls of the AML program and provide prompt notification to FINRA of any changes to the designation;”

CDD RuleThe Financial Crimes Enforcement Network's (FinCEN) final rule on Customer Due Diligence Requirements for Financial Institutions; issued May 11, 2016 to clarify and strengthen customer due diligence for covered financial institutions, including broker-dealers.
✓ “FinCEN, the bureau of the Department of the Treasury responsible for administering the Bank Secrecy Act2 (BSA) and its implementing regulations, issued the CDD Rule3 to clarify and strengthen customer due diligence for covered financial institutions,4 including broker-dealers.”
customer due diligence (four components)FinCEN identifies four components of customer due diligence: (1) customer identification and verification; (2) beneficial ownership identification and verification; (3) understanding the nature and purpose of customer relationships; and (4) ongoing monitoring for reporting suspicious transactions and, on a risk basis, maintaining and updating customer information.
✓ “In its CDD Rule, FinCEN identifies four components of customer due diligence: (1) customer identification and verification; (2) beneficial ownership identification and verification; (3) understanding the nature and purpose of customer relationships; and (4) ongoing monitoring for reporting suspicious transactions and, on a risk basis, maintaining and updating customer information.”
fifth pillarThe requirement, added by the CDD Rule amending the existing AML program rules for covered financial institutions, to explicitly include the third and fourth CDD components (understanding the nature and purpose of customer relationships; and ongoing monitoring) in AML programs.
✓ “The CDD Rule also addresses the third and fourth components by amending the existing AML program rules for covered financial institutions to explicitly require these components to be included in AML programs as a new "fifth pillar."”

Notes: This source item is Division F of the AML Act of 2020 as an uncodified/note-level compilation; most operative program-establishment text (the substance of 31 U.S.C. 5318(h)(1)-(2)) is amended by cross-reference/insertion rather than reproduced in full, so several core program obligations (e.g., the base duty to establish an AML program) are only partially visible in this source and are extracted as given, without filling gaps from the main codified text (out of scope for this item). Per the fidelity rule, ambiguity as to full program content is preserved rather than resolved. Boundary/coordina

Defect Register appendix (123 findings)

defects-cross (26)

D2Divergent definitions of 'financial institution' across statute and AMLA note
31 U.S.C. 5312 defines 'financial institution' as the enumerated list in subsection (a)(2) (banks, brokers/dealers, currency exchanges, casinos, etc.). Section 6003 of AMLA 2020 (captured in 31-usc-5311 and amla-2020-uncodified) defines 'financial institution' as having the meaning in section 5312 BUT expressly expands it to also include (i) an electronic fund transfer network and (ii) a clearing and settlement system. The two definitions materially differ in scope for the same operative term used across the program-establishment regime.
↳ 31-usc-5312 ✓, 31-usc-5311 ✓, amla-2020-uncodified ✓
D2Divergent citation for GLBA 'Federal functional regulator' cross-reference (15 U.S.C. 6809 vs 12 U.S.C. 6809)
The same defined term 'Federal functional regulator' is cross-referenced to section 509 of the Gramm-Leach-Bliley Act, but the U.S. Code citation diverges: 31-usc-5311 and amla-2020-uncodified cite 15 U.S.C. 6809, while 31-usc-5318 cites 12 U.S.C. 6809 in some obligations (OBL-3, and the subsection (h) definition) and 15 U.S.C. 6809 in others (OBL-8, OBL-9, OBL-11, OBL-13). This is a materially inconsistent statutory locator for the same incorporated definition, appearing both across instruments and internally within 31-usc-5318.
↳ 31-usc-5318 ✓, 31-usc-5318 ✓, 31-usc-5311 ✓
D5Dangling reference to superseded 31 CFR part 103 in 31-usc-5318 exemption authority
31 U.S.C. 5318(h)(2)'s exemption authority (OBL-2) references 'part 103 of title 31, of the Code of Federal Regulations, or any successor rule thereto' as the threshold for exempting institutions from minimum program standards. Part 103 was recodified into 31 CFR Chapter X; multiple in-corpus regulations (e.g., 12-cfr-21-occ, 12-cfr-326-353-fdic, 31-cfr-1010) now reference 31 CFR Chapter X. The statutory reference points to a regulation that has been renumbered/relocated, creating a dangling reference (mitigated only by 'or any successor rule').
↳ 31-usc-5318 ✓
D5Dead reference to 31 CFR 103.121 in Federal Reserve BSA compliance program rule
12-cfr-208-fed (Regulation H, state member banks) cross-references the Customer Identification Program implementing regulation as '31 CFR 103.121'. That provision was recodified into 31 CFR Chapter X as 31 CFR 1020.220. The parallel banking-agency rules in the same corpus (12-cfr-21-occ, 12-cfr-326-353-fdic, 12-cfr-748-ncua) all cite 31 CFR 1020.220 for the identical CIP incorporation, confirming the Fed rule's citation is to a superseded/relocated provision.
↳ 12-cfr-208-fed ✓, 12-cfr-326-353-fdic ✓
D5Dead reference to superseded 31 CFR part 103 in Regulation H BSA program rule
12-cfr-208-fed defines the BSA 'compliance program' and its authority by reference to '31 CFR part 103' (twice) — the pre-recodification Treasury regulations. The corpus's other, updated bank-agency program rules (OCC, FDIC, NCUA) all reference '31 CFR Chapter X' for the same implementing regulations. The Regulation H text thus points to a regulation location that has been superseded by 31 CFR Chapter X.
↳ 12-cfr-208-fed ✓, 12-cfr-21-occ ✓
D6CDD 'fifth pillar' incorporated in FinCEN/FINRA program rules but absent from federal banking agency program-establishment rules
FinCEN's CDD Rule added ongoing customer due diligence (understand nature/purpose of relationships; ongoing monitoring; beneficial ownership) as an explicit program element (the 'fifth pillar') in 31 CFR 1020.210, 1023.210, 1022 (via chapter), and FINRA Rule 3310/CAB 331. The federal banking agency program rules (12-cfr-21-occ, 12-cfr-208-fed, 12-cfr-326-353-fdic, 12-cfr-748-ncua) still enumerate only the four classic pillars (internal controls, independent testing, compliance officer, training) plus CIP, and do not include the CDD/ongoing-monitoring element in their text. The banking-agency rules are superseded in substance by the CDD Rule/5318(h) amendments (and the OCC/FDIC/NCUA and Fed NPRMs propose to add CDD to 'mirror FinCEN's existing rule') but the current text has not been revoked/updated to include the fifth pillar.
↳ 12-cfr-21-occ ✓, 31-cfr-1020 ✓, occ-b-2024-19 ✓, FINRA-RN-17-40 ✓
D10Independent-testing frequency threshold mismatch across program instruments
The independent-testing/independent-review pillar carries materially different scope/frequency thresholds across instruments. FINRA Rule 3310 (and FINRA-RN-09-60, FINRA-AML-FAQ) mandate annual (calendar-year) testing, with a biennial alternative only for firms that do not execute customer transactions/hold customer accounts/act as introducing broker. CAB Rule 331 sets independent testing 'no less frequently than every two years.' The MSB rule (31-cfr-1022) and FinCEN guidance set no fixed interval — scope and frequency 'commensurate with the risk.' The federal banking-agency rules and FFIEC guidance state there is 'no regulatory requirement establishing BSA/AML independent testing frequency.' Same pillar, divergent frequency thresholds across the covered sectors.
↳ FINRA-3310 ✓, FINRA-CAB-331 ✓, 31-cfr-1022 ✓, ffiec-man-cp-03 ✓
D10Board-approval and FinCEN-availability program element applies inconsistently across sectors
The program-establishment 'pillars' carry divergent governance requirements across covered sectors. The federal banking-agency rules (OCC, FDIC, NCUA, Fed) require the program be written, board-approved, and minuted. In 31-cfr-1020, only banks LACKING a Federal functional regulator have an explicit board-approval and FinCEN-availability duty; FFR-regulated banks do not by the text's own terms. Broker-dealers (31-cfr-1023, FINRA-3310) require approval by 'senior management,' not the board. MSBs (31-cfr-1022) require only a written program available to Treasury on request, with no board-approval element. Same pillar set, materially different applicability of the approval/availability requirements.
↳ 31-cfr-1020 ✓, 12-cfr-21-occ ✓, FINRA-3310 ✓, 31-cfr-1022 ✓
D10NPRM cites '5318(h)(1)' for banks/broker-dealers but '5318(h)' for MSBs — applicability locator mismatch
Within fincen-program-nprm-2026 the proposed program-rule provisions tie the AML/CFT program requirement to differing statutory subparagraphs: the bank and broker-dealer sections cite '31 U.S.C. 5318(h)(1)' while the MSB section cites '31 U.S.C. 5318(h)' without the subparagraph. The same divergence pattern appears in the current in-force rules (31-cfr-1020 and 31-cfr-1023 deem compliance with '5318(h)(1)'; 31-cfr-1022 references the statutory mandate at the subsection level). This is a scope/locator inconsistency in how the program mandate attaches to different covered sectors.
↳ fincen-program-nprm-2026 ✓, fincen-program-nprm-2026 ✓, 31-cfr-1023 ✓
D3Correspondent-account/private-banking due diligence program duty duplicated across Part 1010 and sector Parts
The affirmative due-diligence-program obligations for correspondent accounts (1010.610), private banking accounts (1010.620), and the foreign shell bank prohibition (1010.630) are stated verbatim in 31-cfr-1010 and again in 31-cfr-1010-230-cdd, and are then incorporated by the bank (31-cfr-1020) and broker-dealer (31-cfr-1023) program rules as mandatory program components. The same operative duty is thus stated in multiple instruments (Part 1010 general provisions and the sector Subpart F/program rules that require compliance with §§1010.610/1010.620).
↳ 31-cfr-1010 ✓, 31-cfr-1010-230-cdd ✓, 31-cfr-1020
D3Four/five program 'pillars' restated in near-identical terms across banking-agency rules
The four minimum program-content requirements (internal controls, independent testing, designated compliance coordinator, training) are stated in materially identical language across four separate federal banking agency regulations (12-cfr-21-occ, 12-cfr-208-fed, 12-cfr-326-353-fdic, 12-cfr-748-ncua) and are further restated in FFIEC guidance and the FinCEN program NPRM. The same duty is imposed by multiple instruments on parallel bank actor classes.
↳ 12-cfr-21-occ ✓, 12-cfr-326-353-fdic ✓, 12-cfr-748-ncua ✓, 12-cfr-208-fed ✓
D4Core program term 'financial institution' undefined in the operative program-establishment statute
31 U.S.C. 5318(h) imposes the base duty that 'each financial institution shall establish anti-money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism programs,' but the term 'financial institution' is not defined within 31-usc-5318 itself (extraction marks it '(undefined here)'). The material scope of the pillars mandate depends on this undefined term, whose definition is supplied only by cross-reference elsewhere (5312, 6003), which as noted diverge.
↳ 31-usc-5318 ✓
D4'reasonably designed risk-based programs' used as the pillars mandate but left undefined in statute
31 U.S.C. 5311 states the statutory purpose of 'preventing the laundering of money and the financing of terrorism through the establishment by financial institutions of reasonably designed risk-based programs,' but neither 'reasonably designed risk-based programs' nor 'money laundering' is defined in the section (both marked '(undefined here)'). This material program-establishment term anchors the entire pillars regime yet lacks a definition in the source that states the purpose.
↳ 31-usc-5311 ✓
D4'covered financial institution' used as duty-holder for due-diligence programs but undefined in 1010 excerpts
The affirmative due-diligence-program obligations in 31-cfr-1010 and 31-cfr-1010-230-cdd bind 'a covered financial institution,' yet the term 'covered financial institution' is marked '(undefined here)' in both items. The scope of these program obligations (which institutions must establish correspondent/private-banking due diligence programs and are subject to the shell-bank prohibition) turns on a material term left undefined in the provided text. The same undefined-term issue recurs in 31-cfr-1010 and amla-2020-uncodified (which notes the definition points to subsection (j)(1) text not reproduced).
↳ 31-cfr-1010-230-cdd ✓, amla-2020-uncodified ✓
D7Broker-dealer pillars obligation assembled only by reading statute, FinCEN rule, and effective SRO rules together
The complete program-establishment obligation for a broker-dealer is not stated in any single instrument. 31 U.S.C. 5318(h) states the base 'each financial institution shall establish' duty but is not sector-specific. 31 CFR 1023.210 deems a broker-dealer to satisfy 5318(h)(1) only if it complies with §§1010.610/1010.620, its Federal functional regulator's rules, AND the rules of its SRO — but only where those SRO rules 'have been made effective under the Securities Exchange Act of 1934 by the appropriate Federal functional regulator in consultation with FinCEN.' The substance of the SRO layer is in FINRA Rule 3310/CAB 331. Thus the full broker-dealer pillars duty is assembled only by reading 31-usc-5318, 31-cfr-1023, and FINRA-3310/FINRA-CAB-331 together.
↳ 31-cfr-1023 ✓, 31-usc-5318 ✓, FINRA-3310 ✓
D7Bank pillars content assembled only across 5318(h), 5318(l), sector CFR, and CIP rule
The full bank program-establishment obligation is scattered: 31 U.S.C. 5318(h) supplies the base program duty; 31 U.S.C. 5318(l) plus 31 CFR 1020.220 supply the mandatory CIP component; the banking-agency rule (e.g., 12 CFR 21.21) supplies the four pillars and cross-incorporates CIP; and §§1010.610/1010.620 supply the due-diligence-program components. No single instrument states the complete bank pillars obligation; it must be assembled by reading 31-usc-5318, 12-cfr-21-occ, 31-cfr-1020, and 31-cfr-1010 together.
↳ 12-cfr-21-occ ✓, 31-cfr-1020 ✓, 31-usc-5318 ✓
D9Obsolete '90-day/July 24, 2002' MSB program-implementation deadline retained in current rule text
31 CFR 1022.210 (O13) still carries the original compliance deadline: 'on or before the later of July 24, 2002, and the end of the 90-day period beginning on the day following the date the business is established.' The July 24, 2002 date is long past and now functions only as an archaic historical artifact within an otherwise current program-establishment rule.
↳ 31-cfr-1022 ✓
D8Obsolete brokerage-account TIN provision limited to accounts opened before October 1, 2003
31 CFR 1023 (OBL-29) imposes a TIN recordkeeping duty tied to 'each brokerage account opened after June 30, 1972 and before October 1, 2003.' The date window is entirely historical; the provision is an archaic recordkeeping rule preserved within the current broker-dealer instrument.

The published priorities are thus superseded-in-substance by the statutory update obligation yet remain the operative published priorities.

(B) has been enacted.' These are dangling internal enumeration references (a paragraph/subparagraph numbering that points to companion items that do not exist).

D10MSB NPRM program-approval clause references 'the bank' rather than the money services business
fincen-program-nprm-2026 notes that the proposed MSB program-approval provision (Sec. 1022.210(d)) refers to approval by 'the money services business's board of directors, an equivalent governing body within the bank' — using 'the bank' as the actor in a provision that should apply to the money services business. ↳ fincen-program-nprm-2026 ✓
D1MSB agent-allocation clause permits allocating responsibility yet imposes non-relievable sole responsibility
31 CFR 1022.210 contains an internal tension between two duties: O14 permits an agent-only MSB and its principal to 'by agreement allocate between them responsibility for development of policies, procedures, and internal controls,' while O15 provides that 'Each money services business shall remain solely responsible for implementation of the requirements' and that nothing relieves any MSB of its obligation. FinCEN guidance (fincen-guid-existing-program-rule-obligations) reinforces the non-relievable duty ('neither the agent nor the principal can avoid liability... by pointing to a contract'). The permissive allocation duty and the non-relievable sole-responsibility duty impose incompatible expectations on the same actors as to who bears program responsibility.
↳ 31-cfr-1022 ✓, 31-cfr-1022 ✓, fincen-guid-existing-program-rule-obligations ✓
D2Divergent 'account' definitions between bank CIP rule and broker-dealer CIP rule
The term 'Account' is defined materially differently for the same CIP pillar across sectors. In 31-cfr-1020 (banks), 'Account' means a formal banking relationship (deposit/credit/safe-deposit/trust, etc.) with enumerated exclusions. In 31-cfr-1023 (broker-dealers), 'Account' means a formal relationship with a broker-dealer to effect securities transactions and hold securities/assets. The definitions reflect different covered relationships for the identical CIP program element.
↳ 31-cfr-1020 ✓, 31-cfr-1023 ✓
D10CIP identity-verification timing differs between bank rule and broker-dealer rule
For the same CIP program pillar, the timing threshold for verifying customer identity differs by sector. The bank rule (31-cfr-1020, OB-1020.220-a2ii) requires verification 'within a reasonable time after the account is opened.' The broker-dealer rule (31-cfr-1023, OBL-13) requires verification 'within a reasonable time before or after the customer's account is opened.' The added 'before' window is a scope/threshold mismatch for the identical CIP verification requirement.
↳ 31-cfr-1020 ✓, 31-cfr-1023 ✓

The same Priorities regime thus applies to materially different institution sets with different supervisory triggers across the two instruments.

The part 103 / 103.121 citations point to a superseded/relocated location.

D2'Account' defined differently for bank CIP vs. broker-dealer CIP

For broker-dealers (§1023.220), 'Account' means a formal relationship with a broker-dealer to effect securities transactions and hold securities/assets for safekeeping or collateral. The scope of relationships each definition covers diverges by sector.

defects-statute (11)

D2Divergent definitions of 'financial institution' across the codified BSA and AMLA 2020 definitional layers
31 U.S.C. 5312 defines 'financial institution' as the enumerated list in subsection (a)(2) (items (A)-(Z)), while 31 U.S.C. 5311 (per AMLA 2020 §6003) and the amla-2020-uncodified item both define 'financial institution' as having the 5312 meaning BUT expanded to include (i) an electronic fund transfer network and (ii) a clearing and settlement system. The AMLA §6003 definition materially broadens the class of covered institutions beyond the 5312 enumeration. This creates a divergent-definition condition where the same term carries a narrower meaning under 5312 and a broader meaning under the §6003/AMLA definitional layer.
